## Conjecture-closing thesis and kill criterion

**Active residual-face thesis.** Proposition 54 closes the projective-Q-bar
branch, and Proposition 55 localizes the remaining matrix obstruction to a
principal set of production-normal players. The next credible positive
producer is therefore a hybrid absorption path: move continuously while the
current zero-payoff face is projective Q, and at a minimal nonprojective normal
face use the ambient standard-Q correction to enter a source-matched finite
product block which returns to a viable face with the same payoff anchor. A
finite concatenation of such continuous pieces and exact blocks would be
decoded by Proposition 54's global opponent-law comparison together with the
checked block consumer. The universal obligation is not merely an LCP
solution: it is a chronological factorization into product rows, with every
row perfect at its actually reached tail and with one common returned payoff.

**Precise obligation now under attack.** Let `P` be a minimal all-normal
principal face on which projective Q fails, and choose a prohibited direction
on `P`. Ambient standard Q forces every standard-LCP correction to use an
owner outside `P`. Section 58 tests whether that cross-face packet is already
semantic data for either a continuous germ, one absorption jump, or a finite
singleton chronology. It is not: the outside owner is off the zero-payoff
face, two positive packet weights force collision mass in a product jump, and
a one-pass singleton schedule pins only the whole packet payoff rather than
each reached tail. The smallest surviving positive statement is consequently
a **source-matched cyclic cross-face block** theorem, not a static packet
theorem.

**Active kill criterion.** Abandon this hybrid route if a minimal all-normal
bad face in an exact standard-Q table admits a uniform positive lower bound on
the sequential-perfection or returned-payoff defect of every source-matched
cross-face product block, including blocks with simultaneous quitters and
arbitrarily small hazards. Failure of one LCP normalization, one product row,
or one ordering is not sufficient: the checked paired-singleton period-two
block already shows that collisions and recurrence can repair all three. If
the universal block factorization is killed, return to the corrected Simon
finite-orbit necessity route below, whose remaining seam is logically
independent of this matrix implementation.

**Universal decision thesis.** The corrected Simon finite-orbit equivalence is
the smallest credible global fork which covers every behavioral quitting
profile. In the hard branch with neither stationarily generated nor instant
approximate equilibria, arbitrarily large variation in every sufficiently
accurate finite `F_epsilon` graph produces approximate equilibria; a bounded
strict Lyapunov certificate at one fixed accuracy would instead produce an
all-behavior terminal gap. After the paper-to-project adapter, the two sides
feed respectively
`quittingGame_exists_uniformEquilibriumPayoff_iff_terminalNash_all_errors`
(`UniformEquilibrium/Quitting/Terminal/TargetTail/TerminalUniformPayoffSelection.lean`)
and
`not_exists_uniformEquilibriumPayoff_iff_exists_terminalExploitabilityGap`
(`UniformEquilibrium/Quitting/Terminal/ExploitabilityGap.lean`). The earlier
charged punishment-floor orbit remains the leading direct positive producer,
but it is no longer the only conjecture-closing language in this notebook.

**Precise universal obligation changed here.** The unproved direction is not
another local root selection: it is the necessity bridge from an arbitrary
behavioral approximate equilibrium to a long finite-dimensional `F_epsilon`
orbit. Simon's printed first-crossing argument silently assumes that survival
cannot jump across a fixed interval. Proposition 42 below supplies a
game-theoretic no-jump bootstrap using min--max floor clipping, support
purification, instant-branch exclusion, and the corrected uniform-`rho`
estimate. This is exactly the point at which arbitrary behavior enters the
finite orbit graph.

**Kill criterion.** Abandon the Simon necessity route if an exact hard-branch
game and approximate-equilibrium profile family defeats Proposition 42's
clipped-row bootstrap after all constants are made uniform, or if the
remaining purified-prefix-to-orbit assembly has a genuine nonvanishing seam
not covered by the existing approximate-path compiler. A raw survival jump is
not such a falsifier: the exact example below shows monotonicity alone fails,
while Proposition 42 uses equilibrium incentives to control that jump. The
charged punishment-floor route remains available if this global bridge fails.

## Exact question

Let `ι` be a nonempty finite player set and let
`r : {S : Finset ι // S.Nonempty} → (ι → ℝ)` be a quitting reward table. Put
`K = ℕ ∪ {∞}` with the one-point compactification topology. For
`t = (t_i)_{i∈ι} ∈ K^ι`, define `m(t) = min_i t_i` and, when `m(t) < ∞`,
`Q(t) = {i : t_i = m(t)}`. Define

`u_i(t) = r(Q(t))_i` if `m(t) < ∞`, and `u_i(t) = 0` otherwise.

The immediate questions are:

1. Is every terminal behavioral profile payoff-equivalent, including for all
   unilateral deviations, to an independent mixed profile on `K`?
2. Is `u_i` continuous off the all-`∞` point, with that point the sole
   compactness defect?
3. Can an approximate-equilibrium existence theorem be proved for this exact
   class of one-singularity games, or can its failure yield an all-behavior
   terminal exploitability gap?

The desired positive conclusion is: for every `ε > 0`, an independent mixed
profile on `K` whose induced terminal behavioral profile is an `ε`-Nash
profile against every behavioral deviation. By
`quittingGame_exists_uniformEquilibriumPayoff_iff_terminalNash_all_errors`,
this would settle the finite-quitting uniform-payoff conjecture. A negative
conclusion must instead produce a fixed positive terminal exploitability gap
for every behavioral profile; failure of a compactness theorem alone does not
do this.

## Why it could matter

This route bypasses the current stopping-law frontier and its two explicit
producer gaps. It tries to isolate all noncompact behavior in one transparent
boundary point of a compact normal-form timing game. The reduction alone is
only a reformulation; it matters only if the special boundary structure admits
a new equilibrium argument or a genuine all-behavior obstruction.

## Work

### 1. Exact planned-time representation

Give `K = ℕ ∪ {∞}` the topology in which finite times are isolated and a
neighborhood of `∞` contains all sufficiently large finite times. A probability
law `q_i` on `K` has tail

`S_i(t) = q_i(∞) + ∑_{s ≥ t} q_i(s)`.

Define its live hazard, when `S_i(t) > 0`, by

`h_i(t) = q_i(t) / S_i(t)`,

and put `h_i(t) = 0` when `S_i(t) = 0`. Since
`S_i(t+1) = S_i(t) - q_i(t)`, induction gives survival through the dates
strictly before `t` equal to `S_i(t)`. Therefore the induced first-stop mass at
`t` is `S_i(t)h_i(t) = q_i(t)`, including the zero-tail case. Continuity from
above for a probability measure on the decreasing tails gives limiting
survival `q_i(∞)`. Thus every law on `K` is induced by a behavioral live-path
hazard. The reverse map is the checked `quittingBehaviorStoppingLaw`.

For independent laws `(q_i)`, the probability that the first quitting
coalition is exactly nonempty `S` at time `t` is

`(∏_{i∈S} q_i(t)) (∏_{j∉S} S_j(t+1))`.

Indeed, the hazard construction first survives to `t`, then the players in
`S` quit and every other player continues. Cancelling the tail denominators
gives exactly the displayed independent planned-time probability. The missing
mass is `∏_i q_i(∞)` and pays zero. Hence the terminal behavioral payoff is
exactly

`U_i(q) = E_q[u_i(T)]`.

The same calculation after replacing one coordinate shows that unilateral
changes of behavior correspond exactly to unilateral changes of the planned-
time law. Conversely every new law is behaviorally implementable by the hazard
above. So terminal Nash and approximate Nash in all behavioral strategies are
equivalent to Nash and approximate Nash in independent laws on `K`. This is
stronger in wording than the checked one-player mixture statement, but uses no
public correlating device: the laws are independent across players.

### 2. Exact topology of the pure timing payoff

At any `t ∈ K^ι` having a finite minimum `m`, choose a neighborhood which fixes
every finite coordinate at or below `m` and forces every `∞` coordinate above
`m`. The minimizing coalition is constant on this neighborhood. Thus every
`u_i` is locally constant off the all-`∞` point.

Every nonempty coalition `S` occurs as a limiting minimizing coalition at the
all-`∞` point: set `t_i=n` for `i∈S` and `t_j=∞` otherwise, then let `n→∞`.
Conversely, finiteness of `ι` lets one pass from any net approaching all-`∞`
to a subnet with constant minimizing coalition. Consequently the punctured
cluster set of coordinate `u_i` at all-`∞` is exactly

`{r(S)_i : S ≠ ∅}`,

while the value at the point is `0`. Its upper and lower semicontinuous
envelopes are therefore respectively

`max ({0} ∪ {r(S)_i : S ≠ ∅})` and
`min ({0} ∪ {r(S)_i : S ≠ ∅})`.

In particular, the all-`∞` point is the only *possible* discontinuity. For one
coordinate it is removable exactly when all that coordinate's coalition
rewards are zero. The vector payoff is continuous there only for the zero
reward table.

For product laws, bounded-payoff integration is continuous at every profile
whose product law assigns zero mass to the pure discontinuity. Equivalently,
`U_i` is continuous at every `(q_j)` for which at least one
`q_j(∞)=0`. This observation alone does not close Nash inequalities, because
after one player deviates to `Never` the remaining opponents may all have
positive `Never` mass. Two distinct zero-`Never` coordinates do suffice for
continuity both on path and after every unilateral deviation. This explains,
without proving a new Lean theorem, the sharp two-seed pattern in the checked
finite-cap bootstrap.

### 3. Exact adjusted escape identity

Fix a deadline `N ≥ 1` and the finite action set

`K_N = {0, …, N-1, ∞}`.

Let `q` be a mixed Nash equilibrium of this finite planned-time game, and let
its hazard realization be the behavioral profile `σ`. For player `i`, write

- `P_i = U_i(q)` for the prescribed payoff;
- `V_i^∞ = U_i(δ_∞,q_{-i})` for the payoff of deviating to `Never`;
- `σ_i = P_i - V_i^∞ ≥ 0` for the finite-game `Never` slack;
- `a_j = q_j(∞)`;
- `E_i = ∏_{j≠i} a_j`, the opponents' probability of jointly reaching the
  deadline; and
- `s_i = r({i})_i`, the singleton self-reward.

**Proposition 3 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** The exact
unrestricted behavioral terminal debt of `σ` at player `i` is

`d_i(σ) = max(0, E_i s_i - σ_i)`.

**Proof.** Against the fixed opponents, every pure time `t < N` and `∞` is a
permitted finite-game deviation and hence has value at most `P_i`. On the other
hand, multilinearity in `q_i` writes `P_i` as the `q_i`-average of those same
finitely many values. Therefore their maximum is exactly `P_i`; this conclusion
does not require every pure action to lie in the support.

For any `t ≥ N`, the opponents have no finite mass at or after `N`. If some
opponent stops before `N`, deviations `t` and `∞` produce the same terminal
coalition. If every opponent selects `∞`, the `∞` deviation pays zero while
the deviation at `t` produces singleton `{i}`. Hence, exactly,

`U_i(δ_t,q_{-i}) = V_i^∞ + E_i s_i`.

This is independent of `t ≥ N`. Behavioral pure-time extremality says the
best-response supremum over all behavioral deviations is the supremum of these
finite times and `Never`. It is consequently

`max(P_i, V_i^∞ + E_i s_i)`.

Subtracting `P_i` and using `σ_i=P_i-V_i^∞` proves the formula. ∎

This identity strengthens the checked bound `d_i ≤ E_i max(0,s_i)`. The raw
charge criterion is sufficient, but it is not necessary. A finite-deadline
profile is an exact unrestricted terminal Nash profile precisely when

`σ_i ≥ E_i s_i` for every player `i`.

For a family of deadlines, terminal approximate Nash follows if the **adjusted
deficits**

`max_i max(0, E_i^{(N)} s_i - σ_i^{(N)})`

tend to zero. This is strictly weaker than asking the raw maximal escape charge
to tend to zero and is accepted by
`quittingGame_exists_uniformEquilibriumPayoff_iff_terminalNash_all_errors`.

### 4. Finite-game producer

For every `N ≥ 1`, the finite planned-time game has a mixed Nash equilibrium by
the ordinary finite-game Nash theorem (and by the checked generic declaration
`exists_isNash_mixed`). Applying the hazard formula in Section 1 to its finite
laws gives a literal behavioral profile which is all-Continue from `N` onward.
The payoff identity and unilateral-law correspondence show that every pure
time below `N`, as well as `Never`, is bounded by the prescribed payoff. Thus
it supplies the mathematical data of `QuittingFiniteDeadlineNashProfile` for
every finite reward table and every deadline.

This producer is ordinary mathematics only at present. The Lean module states
that it does not construct this realization. More importantly, the producer
does not settle the conjecture: Proposition 3 leaves the adjusted-deficit
selection problem.

### 5. Exact small examples

#### A. Raw escape charge can be one while exact debt is zero

Take two players and

- `r({1})=(1,0)`;
- `r({2})=(0,-1)`; and
- `r({1,2})=(0,-1)`.

Let player 1 quit at time `0` and player 2 choose `∞`. This is an exact terminal
Nash profile: player 1 gets `1` and can get at most `1`; player 2 gets `0`, a
time-0 collision gives `-1`, and every later choice leaves player 1's singleton
exit unchanged.

For player 1 at any positive deadline, `E_1=1` and `s_1=1`, so the raw escape
charge is `1`. But `V_1^∞=0`, `P_1=1`, and the `Never` slack is `σ_1=1`.
Proposition 3 gives debt `max(0,1-1)=0`. Thus replacing the raw charge by the
adjusted deficit is a genuine strengthening, not cosmetic algebra.

#### B. A finite-deadline Nash profile may still have positive full debt

Take two players and

- `r({1})=(1,0)`;
- `r({2})=(0,1)`; and
- `r({1,2})=(-1,-1)`.

At deadline `N=1`, let each player independently choose time `0` with
probability `1/2` and `∞` with probability `1/2`. Against this mixture, both
available actions have payoff `0`, so it is an exact Nash equilibrium of the
two-action deadline game. For either player, `E_i=1/2`, `s_i=1`, and
`σ_i=0`; Proposition 3 gives full terminal debt `1/2`. Indeed, deviating to
time `1` earns `0` when the opponent quit at time `0` and earns `1` when the
opponent chose `∞`.

This example refutes the failed implication “finite-deadline mixed Nash
equilibrium implies unrestricted terminal approximate Nash with no residual.”
The strongest surviving statement is the exact adjusted identity.

#### C. Pure-payoff nonclosedness at the singular point

If all players choosing the same finite date gives each payoff `1`, then the
pure profiles `(n,…,n)` have payoff vector `1` for every `n` and converge in
`K^ι` to all-`∞`, whose payoff is `0`. This is the elementary source of the
compactness failure. The checked one-player
`StationarilyGeneratedCompactnessObstruction` gives a stronger equilibrium-
level version under coordinatewise root convergence.

#### D. Exact deadline equilibria with exponentially vanishing deficit

For the table in Example B, at deadline `N` put

`q_i(t)=2^{-(t+1)}` for `0≤t<N`, and `q_i(∞)=2^{-N}`

for both players. Against player `i`, quitting at time `t<N` pays `1` exactly
when the opponent chooses a later time or `∞`, an event of probability
`2^{-(t+1)}`, and pays `-1` exactly when the opponent also chooses `t`, an
event with the same probability. Earlier opponent quits pay `0`. Thus every
finite pure time has value `0`; `Never` also has value `0`. The profile is an
exact mixed Nash equilibrium of the deadline game.

Here `σ_i=0`, `E_i=2^{-N}`, and Proposition 3 gives exact unrestricted debt
`2^{-N}`. This provides terminal approximate Nash profiles at every error and
hence a uniform-equilibrium payoff for this table. It also shows that the
positive debt in the one-stage Example B is not a persistent obstruction.

### 6. A necessary finite-game signature of any genuine counterexample

For deadline `N`, let `NE_N(r)` be the nonempty set of mixed Nash equilibria of
the finite planned-time game. For `q∈NE_N(r)`, define its exact adjusted maximum

`D_N(q)=max_i max(0,E_i(q)s_i-σ_i(q))`.

By Proposition 3, the hazard realization has unrestricted terminal debt
exactly `D_N(q)` in the maximum-player norm.

**Proposition 4 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** Suppose the
quitting game has no uniform-equilibrium payoff. Then there is a fixed `g>0`
such that, for every deadline `N≥1` and every `q∈NE_N(r)`, some player `i`
satisfies

`E_i(q)s_i-σ_i(q) ≥ g`.

For that player necessarily

1. `s_i ≥ g`;
2. `E_i(q) ≥ g/s_i > 0`;
3. every opponent has `q_j(∞) ≥ g/s_i`; and
4. either `q_i(∞)>0`, in which case support indifference gives `σ_i(q)=0`,
   or player `i` is the unique zero-`Never` coordinate.

Consequently, along any sequence of deadlines and any selection of finite-game
equilibria, one fixed positive-solo player satisfies these bounds on an
infinite subsequence.

**Proof.** The checked negative endpoint supplies `g>0` such that every
behavior profile has a unilateral terminal improvement of at least `g`.
Realize arbitrary `q∈NE_N(r)` by Section 4. Its maximum semantic debt is at
least `g`, while Proposition 3 says that maximum is `D_N(q)`. This proves the
displayed inequality for some `i`.

Since `0≤E_i≤1` and `σ_i≥0`, the inequality implies `s_i≥g` and
`E_i≥g/s_i`. Every factor `q_j(∞)` in the product `E_i` lies in `[0,1]`, so
each is at least the product and hence at least `g/s_i`. If `q_i(∞)>0`, the
pure action `∞` belongs to player `i`'s support; mixed-Nash indifference gives
`V_i^∞=P_i`, hence `σ_i=0`. If `q_i(∞)=0`, all opponents have strictly positive
`Never` mass, making `i` the unique zero coordinate. Finally, finite
pigeonhole selection fixes `i` on an infinite subsequence. ∎

This is a conjecture-facing counterexample screen, not a counterexample. Any
genuine failure must survive **every** finite deadline and **every** equilibrium
selection with a uniform positive adjusted deficit, while keeping all opponents
of the witnessing player uniformly at `Never`. Conversely, finding any
sequence `(N_k,q_k)` with `D_{N_k}(q_k)→0` proves terminal approximate Nash
existence at every error and therefore a uniform-equilibrium payoff.

### 7. A coherent deadline selection and its exact debt recursion

Arbitrary Nash equilibria at different deadlines need not be related. There is,
however, a canonical *kind* of coherent selection obtained by backward
induction. Put `x_0=0`. Given a continuation vector `x_n`, choose a mixed Nash
root `p_n` of the finite one-stage quitting game whose all-Continue outcome has
payoff `x_n`, and let

`x_(n+1)=quittingRootSuccessorPayoff(r,x_n,p_n)`.

Finite mixed-Nash existence supplies such a root at every step. The
`(n+1)`-stage profile uses `p_n` first, followed by the already constructed
`n`-stage profile, and then all-Continue forever. Backward induction makes this
a finite-deadline Nash profile. Thus this single infinite Nash--Bellman orbit
selects one equilibrium coherently for every deadline.

Fix player `i`. For the `n`-stage profile define

- `W_i^n`: payoff to `i` from deviating to `Never`;
- `A_i^n`: probability that every opponent chooses `Never` through the
  deadline;
- `B_i^n=W_i^n+A_i^n s_i`: payoff from quitting just after the deadline;
- `δ_i^n=B_i^n-x_n(i)`: the signed adjusted escape deficit; and
- `d_i^n=max(0,δ_i^n)`: the exact unrestricted debt from Proposition 3.

At the newly prepended root `p_n`, let

`c_i^n=∏_{j≠i} p_n(j)(Continue)`

and let `C_i^n` be player `i`'s one-stage payoff from choosing Continue at
that root against `p_n(-i)`, with continuation `x_n`. Root Nash gives the
nonnegative Continue slack

`ell_i^n=x_(n+1)(i)-C_i^n ≥ 0`.

**Proposition 5 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** For every
player and every `n`,

`δ_i^(n+1)=c_i^n δ_i^n-ell_i^n`.

Consequently `d_i^(n+1)≤d_i^n`, so the exact maximum terminal debt of this
coherent finite-deadline selection is nonincreasing.

**Proof.** If `w_i^n` is the expected current reward on opponent-only quitting
outcomes when `i` Continues, then

`W_i^(n+1)=w_i^n+c_i^n W_i^n`

and

`A_i^(n+1)=c_i^n A_i^n`.

Therefore `B_i^(n+1)=w_i^n+c_i^n B_i^n`. On the other hand,
`C_i^n=w_i^n+c_i^n x_n(i)`. Subtraction from `x_(n+1)(i)` gives the displayed
recursion. If `δ_i^n≤0`, its successor is nonpositive. If `δ_i^n>0`, then
`δ_i^(n+1)≤c_i^nδ_i^n≤δ_i^n`. Taking positive parts proves monotonicity. ∎

Since the player set is finite, `D_n=max_i d_i^n` decreases to a limit
`D_∞≥0`. If `D_∞=0`, this coherent selection itself supplies terminal
approximate Nash profiles at every error and proves uniform-payoff existence.

The alternative `D_∞>0` has rigid consequences. Choose `i` with
`d_i^n↓d_i^∞=D_∞>0`. Since `δ_i^0=s_i`, necessarily `s_i>0`, and unrolling the
recursion gives

`A_i^n=∏_{k<n}c_i^k ≥ D_∞/s_i`.

Thus

`∑_n (1-c_i^n)<∞`,

and, because each opponent's quit probability at root `p_n` is at most
`1-c_i^n`, every opponent of `i` has summable quit probabilities along the
reverse Nash--Bellman orbit. Also, since every `A_i^n` is bounded below,
division of the recursion by `A_i^(n+1)` yields

`δ_i^(n+1)/A_i^(n+1)
 =δ_i^n/A_i^n-ell_i^n/A_i^(n+1)`.

Hence `∑_n ell_i^n<∞` and in particular `ell_i^n→0`.

This is more precise than an arbitrary nonclosed limit. Failure of the coherent
selection forces one fixed positive-solo player whose opponents approach
all-Continue with summable total quitting, while that player's local Continue
action becomes asymptotically optimal. What is still missing is a theorem that
turns this summable-opponent boundary into a terminal approximate equilibrium,
or an exact reward table showing that the positive limit can persist for every
possible coherent Nash-root selection.

### 8. Exact obstruction: arbitrary coherent selection can keep positive debt

The final alternative in Section 7 cannot be ruled out for an *arbitrary*
coherent Nash-root selection, even in a solved two-player game. Let

- `r({1})=(1,0)`;
- `r({2})=(2,0)`; and
- `r({1,2})=(0,0)`.

At continuation `x_0=(0,0)`, choose the one-stage Nash root where player 1
Continues, while player 2 Quits with probability `1/2` and Continues with
probability `1/2`. Player 1's prescribed payoff is `1`; deviating to Quit gives
`(1/2)·0+(1/2)·1=1/2`. Player 2 receives zero under every action. Thus the root
is Nash and its successor value is `x_1=(1,0)`.

At continuation `(1,0)`, the all-Continue root is Nash: player 1's solo Quit
payoff is exactly its continuation value `1`, and player 2's is exactly `0`.
Choose this all-Continue root at every later recursion step. Then
`x_n=(1,0)` for all `n≥1`.

The resulting deadline-`N` planned-time equilibrium has player 1 choose
`Never`; player 2 chooses time `N-1` with probability `1/2` and `Never` with
probability `1/2`. It is directly Nash in the finite timing game:

- player 1 gets `1`; quitting before `N-1` also gives singleton payoff `1`,
  quitting at `N-1` gives `1/2`, and `Never` gives `1`;
- player 2 always gets `0`.

But a player-1 deviation to any time `t≥N` gets `2` when player 2 quit at
`N-1` and gets singleton payoff `1` when player 2 chose `Never`, for expected
payoff `3/2`. Hence the exact unrestricted debt is `1/2` at every deadline.
In Proposition 5, `c_1^0=1/2`, `ell_1^0=0`, and thereafter
`c_1^n=1`, `ell_1^n=0`, so `δ_1^n=1/2` for all `n≥1`.

This persistent boundary is a property of the selection, not the game. Choose
instead at every recursion step the root where player 1 Continues and player 2
Quits with probability `1/2`. Then

`x_n(1)=2(1-2^{-n})`, `x_n(2)=0`,

and the deadline profile gives player 2 geometric quit-time mass
`2^{-(t+1)}` with `Never` mass `2^{-N}`. Its exact adjusted debt is `2^{-N}`.
The infinite stationary version has player 2 eventually Quit almost surely,
pays `(2,0)`, and is an exact terminal Nash profile: player 1's pure quit-time
payoff approaches `2` from below, while player 2 is always indifferent.

There is also a one-step optimal choice: at the first root let player 2 Quit
surely. This is Nash, gives payoff `(2,0)`, and makes player 1's opponent-
`Never` factor zero immediately. This reinforces that the example obstructs
universal selection, not existential debt minimization.

Therefore the following tempting statement is false:

> Every coherent backward-induction equilibrium selection has adjusted debt
> tending to zero.

The strongest surviving selection target is existential: choose the Nash root
at each continuation so that the adjusted debt vanishes. Compactness of the
state orbit alone cannot make this choice, because the bad orbit becomes
constant at the phantom all-Continue continuation `(1,0)`.

### 9. The weakest local debt-descent selection objective

The recursion in Proposition 5 gives an exact local classification. The
interpretable accounts are `(x,W,A)`, but the recursion only needs the smaller
state `(x,δ)`: the Nash-root set depends on `x`, and Proposition 5 determines
`δ'` from `(x,δ,p)`. Thus `(x,δ)` is the minimal augmented state used below.
Write

`D=max_i max(0,δ_i)` and `I_max={i:δ_i=D}`,

assuming `D>0`. For a Nash root `p`, call a player a quitter when
`p_i(Quit)>0`.

**Proposition 6 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** The successor
maximum debt is strictly below `D` in either of the following cases:

1. at least two players are quitters; or
2. exactly one player `k` is a quitter, and either `k∉I_max` or its Continue
   slack `ell_k` is positive.

The only roots which can preserve `D` are:

- the all-Continue root; and
- a one-quitter root owned by some `k∈I_max` with `ell_k=0`.

**Proof.** With two distinct quitters, every player has a quitting opponent,
so `c_i<1` for every `i`. Proposition 5 then strictly contracts every positive
`δ_i`; nonpositive coordinates stay nonpositive. Finiteness of the player set
makes the new maximum strictly smaller than `D`.

With sole quitter `k`, every `i≠k` has `c_i<1`, while `c_k=1`. Thus all
maximizers other than possibly `k` contract. Coordinate `k` obeys
`δ'_k=δ_k-ell_k`, which preserves `D` exactly only when `k∈I_max` and
`ell_k=0`. The no-quitter case is the all-Continue root; then `c_i=1` and
`ell_i=0` for every player, so every deficit is unchanged. ∎

This proposition identifies the weakest *one-step* objective: whenever the
Nash-root correspondence contains a root outside the two preserving classes,
select one which minimizes successor `D` and obtain strict progress. In the bad
selection of Section 8, the all-Continue root preserves player 1's debt, while
the alternative root owned by player 2 falls under case 2 and reduces it.

Local greedy minimization is not yet a compositional theorem. A smaller
immediate debt could move the continuation to a state with worse future Nash
options. The exact compositional objective is the finite-horizon value

`m_0(z)=D(z)`,

`m_(k+1)(z)=min_{p∈NashRoots(x)} m_k(T(z,p))`,

where `z=(x,δ)` and `T` is the continuous augmented transition above. The
Nash-root set is nonempty and compact, so every displayed minimum is attained.
Equivalently, `m_k(z)` is the least final adjusted debt among all length-`k`
coherent backward-induction selections from `z`. Extending an optimal path by
any Nash root and using Proposition 5 shows `m_(k+1)(z_0)≤m_k(z_0)` at the
initial state `z_0=(0,s)`, since `δ_i^0=s_i`.

Thus the direct selection problem has an exact scalar form:

`inf_k m_k(z_0)=0`.

If true for every reward table, it supplies terminal approximate Nash profiles
and solves the conjecture. If the infimum is positive, compact finite-prefix
selection gives an infinite augmented Nash--Bellman path with persistent debt,
and Proposition 6 forces its asymptotic choices into all-Continue or tight
one-owner roots. A proof still needs either to compile that tight one-owner
boundary or to show that a debt-decreasing Nash branch remains selectable.

A delicate topological issue is now visible: the Nash-root correspondence has
a closed graph but need not be lower hemicontinuous. Even if a limiting phantom
state admits a positive-charge debt-decreasing root, such a root need not
persist at nearby states. Therefore “at the limit choose the better root” is
not justified without a stable-branch or semialgebraic selection theorem.

The table in Section 8 gives an exact instance. At continuation
`x*=(1,0)`, the root where player 1 Continues and player 2 mixes Quit/Continue
with probabilities `1/2,1/2` is Nash and decreases player 1's debt. Approach
`x*` through `x_m=(1,1/m)`. Player 2's Quit payoff is always `0`, whereas if
player 1 Continues its Continue payoff is `1/m>0`; hence no Nash roots near
`x_m` can converge to the displayed mixed-player-2 root. A possible attempt to
restore player-2 indifference by changing player 1 requires player 1 to Quit
surely, which does not converge to player 1's Continue action at `x*`.

Thus the debt-decreasing branch is born only at the limit. This is a minimal
exact obstruction to any proof which uses closed graph plus a limiting root
and then assumes that root can be selected along the approximating orbit.

### 10. Conditional compilation of a persistent tight-solo boundary

Proposition 6 suggests a narrower boundary calculation. Suppose that along a
tail of a coherent selection every non-all-Continue root has exactly one
quitter, the same owner `k`, with quit probability `h_n>0`; suppose these roots
preserve `k`'s positive maximal deficit, and `∑_n h_n=∞`.

Preservation gives `ell_k^n=0`. Since every opponent Continues, player `k`'s
Continue payoff is `x_n(k)`, while the successor coordinate is

`x_(n+1)(k)=(1-h_n)x_n(k)+h_n s_k`.

Thus `h_n>0` and `ell_k^n=0` force `x_n(k)=s_k`. For an outsider `j`,

`x_(n+1)(j)=(1-h_n)x_n(j)+h_n r({k})_j`.

The divergent hazard sum makes the corresponding survival product vanish, so
`x_n(j)→r({k})_j`. Passing the root-Nash inequality for outsider `j` to the
limit requires one further split. At the `n`th positive-hazard root that
inequality is exactly

`h_n r({j,k})_j+(1-h_n)s_j≤x_(n+1)(j)`.

If the hazards have a positive cluster point `h_*>0`, the limit is already
the stationary root inequality at hazard `h_*`. If instead `h_n→0`, the
display gives `s_j≤r({k})_j`. (All-Continue roots may be deleted from this
subsequence because they change neither `x` nor `δ`.)

There is one exact repair under a sharp extra condition.

**Proposition 7 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** In the setting
above, assume additionally that every outsider tied at its solo value satisfies

`s_j=r({k})_j  →  r({j,k})_j≤r({k})_j`.

Then the game has an exact stationary terminal Nash profile in which only
`k` Quits with one fixed positive probability.

**Proof.** First suppose a subsequence has `h_n→h_*>0`. Passing the exact
outsider inequality above to the limit gives

`h_* r({j,k})_j+(1-h_*)s_j≤r({k})_j`

for every outsider. Thus the desired stationary root exists with hazard
`h_*`, without using the extra equality condition.

It remains to consider `h_n→0`. Then `s_j≤r({k})_j` for every outsider.
Choose a small `h>0`. If `s_j<r({k})_j`, then for sufficiently small `h` the
strict inequality persists in

`h r({j,k})_j+(1-h)s_j≤r({k})_j`.

If `s_j=r({k})_j`, the added hypothesis gives the same inequality for every
`h∈[0,1]`. Finiteness of the outsider set permits one common positive `h`.

Now let `k` Quit independently with stationary probability `h` and every
outsider Continue. Absorption by `k` occurs almost surely, so the prescribed
terminal payoff is `r({k})`. Owner `k` is indifferent between Continue and
Quit because `r({k})_k=s_k`; choosing `Never` gives zero, which is no better
because the persistent positive deficit implies `s_k>0`. For outsider `j`,
waiting gives `r({k})_j`, while
quitting at a live stage gives exactly the left side of the displayed
inequality. The one-stage inequality propagates over every pure quit time; or
equivalently the stationary Bellman root is exact Nash at its fixed
continuation. Behavioral pure-time extremality then gives exact Nash against
all behavioral deviations. ∎

Therefore a divergent single-owner tight boundary is positively compilable
unless some outsider has the exact equality--collision obstruction

`s_j=r({k})_j<r({j,k})_j`.

This does not finish the optimizer argument. A persistent optimal path may
retain only summable total absorption, or may approach the preserving classes
without entering an exact one-owner tail. But it identifies the precise local
data which can prevent the simplest solo stationary repair; strict outsider
inequality is not part of the hard residual case.

### 11. An exact equality--collision corridor traps every finite optimizer

The residual equality--collision case creates more than a failure of lower
hemicontinuity. Consider two players `k,j` and put

`a=r({j,k})_j-s_j>0`,

`b=r({j,k})_k-r({j})_k<0`,

while `r({k})_j=s_j`. At the continuation

`x(y)=(s_k,s_j+y)`, with `y>0`,

write `p` for `k`'s Quit probability and `q` for `j`'s. Player `k`'s
Quit-minus-Continue endpoint difference is exactly `qb`, while player `j`'s
is

`p a-(1-p)y`.

**Proposition 8 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** The Nash-root
set at `x(y)` is exactly

`q=0` and `0≤p≤y/(a+y)`.

Every non-all-Continue root is therefore a sole-`k` tight root. Its successor
is `x(y')` with `y'=(1-p)y>0`. Consequently, if `k` owns a positive maximal
signed deficit at such an augmented state, every coherent selection of every
finite length preserves that maximum debt exactly. At the limiting state
`x(0)`, however, every root `p=0`, `0<q≤1` is Nash and strictly contracts
`k`'s positive deficit.

**Proof.** If `q>0`, then `b<0` makes Continue the unique best response of
`k`, so `p=0`. But at `p=0` the endpoint difference of `j` is `-y<0`, forcing
`q=0`, a contradiction. Hence every Nash root has `q=0`. Player `k` is then
indifferent, while Continue is a best response for `j` exactly when

`p a-(1-p)y≤0`,

which is the displayed interval. Conversely these conditions make both
players best respond, so the interval is exact. The successor calculation is

`x'_k=s_k`,

`x'_j=p r({k})_j+(1-p)x_j=s_j+(1-p)y`.

Thus every finite successor remains inside the positive corridor. Such a root
has `c_k=1` and zero Continue slack for `k`, so Proposition 5 preserves
`delta_k`; all other positive deficits are nonincreasing. At `y=0`, player
`j` is indifferent when `p=0`, while `q>0` makes `k` strictly prefer Continue
because `b<0`. Hence the stated limiting roots are Nash. Since `j` is then the
sole quitter, `c_k=1-q<1`, and Proposition 5 strictly contracts a positive
`delta_k`. ∎

The smallest exact numerical table is

- `r({k})=(1,0)`;
- `r({j})=(2,0)`; and
- `r({j,k})=(0,1)`,

with coordinates ordered `(k,j)`. Here `a=1`, `b=-2`, and the corridor is
`x(y)=(1,y)`, whose roots are exactly `q=0` and
`0≤p≤y/(1+y)`. Choosing the largest permitted hazard gives

`1/y_(n+1)=1/y_n+1`,

so `y_n` tends to zero and the total `k` hazard diverges, but the active debt
is unchanged at every finite stage.

This example does **not** refute the global initial-state selection objective:
the artificial continuation `x(y)` is not the zero terminal continuation, and
the same reward table has other good roots from `x=0`. It does refute a
compositional induction claiming `inf_m m_m(z)=0` for every augmented state.
It also shows why allowing an infinite limiting orbit and then changing roots
at its limit is a genuinely stronger operation than any finite-horizon dynamic
program. A successful global proof must use reachability from `z_0`, compile
the whole limiting orbit directly, or attach an ordinal/transfinite closure;
pointwise finite-horizon debt minimization is insufficient.

### 12. A global all-equilibrium obstruction to the finite-deadline route

The corridor becomes reachable asymptotically after changing only the
outsider's solo level. Take two active players `k,j` with

- `r({k})=(1,-1)`;
- `r({j})=(2,-1)`; and
- `r({k,j})=(0,0)`,

again in coordinate order `(k,j)`. Let `(u_n,v_n)` be the continuation payoff
of the unique `n`-stage backward-induction equilibrium, starting from
`(u_0,v_0)=(0,0)`. At a root with tail `(u,v)`, write `p` for `k`'s Quit
probability and `q` for `j`'s. The two endpoint differences are

`Delta_k=(1-u)-q(3-u)`,

`Delta_j=p(2+v)-(1+v)`.

Throughout the orbit `u<1` and `v>-1`, so the one-stage game has the unique
interior Nash root

`p=(1+v)/(2+v)`,

`q=(1-u)/(3-u)`.

Endpoint indifference gives the successor recursion

`u'=2/(3-u)`,

`v'=-1/(2+v)`.

Solving it exactly yields

`u_n=1-1/(2^(n+1)-1)`,

`v_n=-1+1/(n+1)`,

and the root prepended to an `n`-stage tail has

`p_n=1/(n+2)`,

`q_n=1/(2^(n+2)-1)`.

Thus the `k` hazards have divergent sum, while the opponent-`j` hazards are
summable. In the `N`-stage equilibrium the `Never` masses are

`a_k^(N)=product_(n<N)(1-p_n)=1/(N+1)`,

`a_j^(N)=product_(n<N)(1-q_n)=2^N/(2^(N+1)-1)`.

Player `k` has positive `Never` mass and hence zero finite-game `Never` slack.
Proposition 3 therefore gives exact unrestricted debt

`d_k^(N)=a_j^(N)=2^N/(2^(N+1)-1) -> 1/2`.

Player `j` has negative solo reward, so its adjusted debt is zero.

**Proposition 9 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** For every
finite deadline `N`, the displayed equilibrium is the **only** mixed Nash
equilibrium of the finite timing game. Consequently

`inf_(q in NE_N(r)) D_N(q)=2^N/(2^(N+1)-1)≥1/2`.

The same conclusion holds after padding by any finite set of at least two
dummy players, so the obstruction occurs in the open cardinal range `|I|≥4`.

**Proof of uniqueness.** First consider the two active players. At any reached
live root, all-Continue must have positive probability. If `k` Quits surely,
then `j` strictly prefers to Quit simultaneously (`0>-1`), after which `k`
strictly prefers to Continue (`2>0`), a contradiction. If `j` Quits surely,
then `k` strictly prefers Continue, giving `j` payoff `-1`. Against any finite
tail law of `k`, player `j` can obtain strictly more than `-1`: if `k` has
positive `Never` mass, choosing `Never` does so; otherwise some finite time
has positive atom, and quitting at that time replaces payoff `-1` by `0` on
that atom. Thus `j` cannot Quit surely either.

The next live subgame is consequently reached with positive probability, so a
Nash profile restricts to a Nash profile there. Backward induction now applies
without an equilibrium-refinement assumption. The last root is the unique
interior root above at tail zero; inductively, every earlier root has the
displayed tail `(u_n,v_n)` and the unique interior root `(p_n,q_n)`. This
proves uniqueness and the formulas. The product for `a_j^(N)` telescopes:

`product_(n<N) (1-1/(2^(n+2)-1))=2^N/(2^(N+1)-1)`.

For padding, let `D` be any finite dummy set. Active-player rewards ignore
dummies and use the table above when the quitting coalition contains an active
player; they are zero on dummy-only exits. A dummy receives `-1` whenever it
belongs to the quitting coalition and zero otherwise. Choosing `Never` gives
each dummy zero, so no equilibrium can give positive probability to a dummy
being in the first quitting coalition.

The preceding sure-exit contradiction remains valid with dummies. In the
`j`-sure case, continuing past the root either exposes a dummy-only exit, worth
zero and hence already strictly better than `-1`, or reduces to a finite law
of `k`, where the positive-atom argument applies. Hence every live root still
has positive all-Continue probability. A dummy putting positive mass on any
finite live time would then have positive probability of being a first
quitter and negative expected payoff, contradicting its zero `Never`
deviation. Thus every dummy is `Never`, every live subgame is reached, and the
active equilibrium is exactly the unique two-player recursion. Dummy factors
equal one in `E_k`, so the adjusted debt is unchanged. ∎

This is the promised exact reward-table obstruction to the finite-deadline
selection theorem. It is stronger than Proposition 8: selection cannot help,
because **every** finite timing Nash equilibrium has debt at least `1/2`, at
every deadline, even after padding into the conjecture's cardinal range. The
obstruction is precisely the summable-opponent boundary from Proposition 5:
`j`'s total hazard is finite, while `k`'s hazard is divergent and the orbit
converges to `(1,-1)`.

It is not an all-behavior counterexample and does not refute the uniform-
equilibrium conjecture. The underlying two-player game has a uniform-
equilibrium payoff by
`quittingGame_exists_uniformEquilibriumPayoff_of_card_eq_two`
(`UniformEquilibrium/Quitting/Classification/PlayerReindex.lean`), and the
dummy extension preserves it by assigning every dummy all-Continue. By the
checked positive endpoint equivalence, terminal approximate Nash profiles
therefore exist; Proposition 9 proves that they cannot in general be obtained
as Nash equilibria of finite timing games with a hard all-Continue tail.
Further progress along the quit-time route must change the boundary condition,
use approximate rather than exact finite-game Nash, or compile an infinite
nonstationary orbit directly.

### 13. Smallest replacement: a diffuse refusal-punishment boundary

The exact table in Proposition 9 suggests a boundary condition which is both
strictly weaker than a Nash continuation and sufficient for terminal
approximate Nash. Choose distinct players `o,k`. Player `o` is the scheduled
owner and `k` is its refusal punisher. Assume

1. for every outsider `i≠o`,

   `r({o,i})_i≤r({o})_i`; and

2. `r({k})_o≤s_o=r({o})_o`.

No condition is imposed on the collision reward `r({o,k})_o`. Put

`Delta=max(0,r({o,k})_o-s_o)`.

For an integer `L≥1`, define a behavioral profile as follows. At date zero,
`o` Quits surely and every outsider Continues. If, off the prescribed path,
everyone Continues at date zero, player `k` draws a private uniform planned
quit time from `{1,...,L}` and all other prescribed players Continue forever.
Section 1's hazard construction realizes this uniform law behaviorally.

**Proposition 10 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** The prescribed
terminal payoff is the fixed vector `r({o})`, and the displayed profile is a
`Delta/L`-terminal Nash profile against every behavioral unilateral deviation.
Consequently `r({o})` is a uniform-equilibrium payoff.

**Proof.** A deviating outsider `i≠o` cannot act before date zero. Continuing
lets the scheduled owner exit alone and pays `r({o})_i`; Quitting at date zero
creates exactly `{o,i}` and pays no more by hypothesis 1. The game has already
absorbed after either action, so every later component of the deviation is
irrelevant.

Now let `o` deviate. Quitting at date zero gives `s_o`, its prescribed
coordinate. If it Continues, the uniform punishment clock starts. Against a
pure quit time `t∈{1,...,L}`, the outcome is `{k}` when the punishment time is
earlier, `{o,k}` on the unique matching atom, and `{o}` when it is later. The
first and third rewards to `o` are at most `s_o`; the matching atom has
probability `1/L`. Hence the payoff is at most `s_o+Delta/L`. Quitting after
the punishment window or choosing `Never` gives `r({k})_o≤s_o`.

Behavioral pure-time extremality upgrades this bound from deterministic times
to every history-dependent behavioral deviation of `o`. Thus the maximum
terminal debt is at most `Delta/L`. Taking arbitrarily large `L` gives terminal
approximate Nash profiles whose terminal payoff is literally the same target
`r({o})`. For every requested `epsilon>0`, choose `L` with
`Delta/L≤epsilon`; then
`quittingGame_isUniformEquilibriumPayoff_of_terminalTargetAcceptance` supplies
the named uniform-payoff conclusion. ∎

This is the smallest replacement theorem not refuted by Proposition 9. It
does not require the off-path punishment tail to be Nash, floor-admissible, or
close to the hard-deadline tail. It prices the unique refusal deviation by
diffusing the collision atom over a longer window. A narrow search for
`diffuse`, `refusal`, `collision punishment`, `scheduled quit`, and `finite
window` found extensive general diffuse-clock infrastructure, notably under
`UniformEquilibrium/Quitting/Cycles/ConditionedDiffuse*`, but no inspected
declaration was used here and no novelty over that broader machinery is
claimed.

For Proposition 9 take `o=j` and `k=k`. The outsider inequality is
`r({j,k})_k=0≤2=r({j})_k`, the refusal payoff is
`r({k})_j=-1=s_j`, and `Delta=1`. The construction gives explicit terminal
`1/L`-Nash profiles with fixed payoff `(2,-1)`, while Proposition 9 says every
exact hard-deadline Nash profile has debt at least `1/2`. With the dummy
padding, each dummy's date-zero collision reward is `-1≤0`, so the same
construction works verbatim in every player count at least four.

The sharp remaining boundary of Proposition 10 is also explicit. It does not
handle an outsider who profits by joining the scheduled date-zero coalition,
and one punisher may fail to hold the owner's refusal payoff below `s_o`.
Randomizing the scheduled owner's date could diffuse outsider collisions but
would also let outsiders preempt, so that extension needs new inequalities
rather than a silent reuse of the proof.

### 14. Two-window diffusion and a deterministic punishment coalition

The preceding boundary can be extended without public correlation. Assume
first that distinct `o,k` satisfy

`s_i≤r({o})_i` for every `i≠o`,

and `r({k})_o≤s_o`. Define

`Gamma=max_(i≠o) max(0,r({o,i})_i-r({o})_i)`,

`Delta=max(0,r({o,k})_o-s_o)`.

For integers `L,H≥1`, let `o` privately choose a uniform quit time in the
owner window `{0,...,L-1}`. If the game is still live at date `L`, which is an
off-path public refusal signal, let `k` privately choose a uniform quit time in
the punishment window `{L,...,L+H-1}`. Everyone else Continues forever.

**Proposition 11 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** This profile
has the fixed terminal payoff `r({o})` and is an

`max(Gamma/L,Delta/H)`-terminal Nash profile

against all behavioral unilateral deviations. Hence `r({o})` is a uniform-
equilibrium payoff.

**Proof.** Fix outsider `i`. Against a pure quit time inside the owner window,
owner `o` is earlier with some probability and pays `r({o})_i`, `i` is earlier
with some probability and gets `s_i≤r({o})_i`, and equality occurs at one
owner atom of mass `1/L`. Only that atom can improve on `r({o})_i`, by at most
`Gamma/L`. A time after the owner window, or `Never`, sees `o` exit earlier
with probability one. Thus every outsider pure-time gain is at most
`Gamma/L`.

For owner `o`, every quit time inside its own window produces singleton payoff
`s_o`. If it refuses through the window, the same calculation against `k`'s
uniform punishment clock gives at most `s_o+Delta/H`: quitting before `k`
gives `s_o`, tying uses one atom of mass `1/H`, and quitting after `k` or
choosing `Never` gives `r({k})_o≤s_o`. Pure-time extremality proves the claimed
behavioral bound. The fixed-target terminal-to-uniform theorem then gives the
last conclusion. ∎

The two random clocks are private and are never jointly needed on the
prescribed path: the punishment clock starts only after public survival through
the owner window. Thus the construction does not smuggle in a public lottery.
It also keeps the horizon-uniform quantifiers explicit: for every requested
terminal error `epsilon>0`, choose finite `L,H` large enough, obtain one
behavior profile with the same target `r({o})`, and then invoke the checked
fixed-target acceptance compiler.

There is a genuinely multi-punisher alternative when no singleton is a useful
diffuse punisher. Let `P` be a nonempty coalition not containing `o`, and
assume

`r(P)_o≤s_o` and `r(P union {o})_o≤s_o`.

Use the same diffuse owner window, but after refusal let every member of `P`
Quit surely and simultaneously at date `L`.

**Proposition 12 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** Under the
outsider solo inequalities `s_i≤r({o})_i`, this profile has fixed payoff
`r({o})` and terminal debt at most `Gamma/L` against all behavioral
deviations. Consequently `r({o})` is a uniform-equilibrium payoff.

**Proof.** Outsider deviations are unchanged from Proposition 11. Owner quit
times inside the diffuse window give `s_o`; Quitting at the punishment date
gives `r(P union {o})_o≤s_o`; and every later time or `Never` gives
`r(P)_o≤s_o`. Pure-time extremality again completes the proof. ∎

Proposition 12 uses deterministic simultaneous punishment and hence needs no
correlated randomization among the members of `P`. Its two displayed
inequalities are essential for this literal construction: the first prices
refusal, while the second prevents the owner from predictably joining the
punishment coalition. Proposition 11 is complementary: it can tolerate a
profitable owner--punisher collision by diffusing its time, but requires one
singleton punisher with low refusal payoff.

These results isolate a finite actual-data boundary, not an all-game theorem.
They cover exactly the two local failures left by Proposition 10—outsider
joining and owner refusal—whenever either a singleton diffuse punisher or a
deterministic punitive coalition exists. The unresolved case has both:

- some outsider has solo payoff above `r({o})_i` for every plausible owner,
  so it can profitably preempt a diffuse owner clock; and
- every available punishment plan either rewards refusal or has a predictable
  owner-collision gain.

Any further clock construction must couple several scheduled owners or use a
cycle of punishments; independent diffusion alone does not erase a positive
preemption baseline.

### 15. Rare-renewal singleton lotteries solve a positive-preemption class

A finite renewal cycle can replace the missing anchor row. Choose distinct
scheduled owners `o_1,...,o_m`, with `m≥2`, and positive weights
`w_1,...,w_m` summing to one. Define the fixed singleton-mixture target

`v_i=sum_a w_a r({o_a})_i`.

Assume the following actual-table conditions:

1. every scheduled owner is indifferent at the mixture,

   `v_(o_a)=s_(o_a)` for every `a`; and

2. every unscheduled player has no profitable solo preemption,

   `s_i≤v_i` for `i` outside `{o_1,...,o_m}`.

Equivalently, `s_i≤v_i` for every player, with equality on the support of the
owner lottery. Put

`kappa=min_a (1-w_a)>0`

and let `M` bound the absolute coalition rewards.

For `0<c≤1/2`, repeat rounds with phases `1,...,m`. At phase `a`, conditional
on survival through the earlier phases of that round, only `o_a` may Quit,
with probability

`alpha_a=c w_a/(1-c sum_(b<a)w_b)`.

If nobody Quits, start a new round. The denominator is at least `1-c`, so this
is a legal probability. From the beginning of a round, the exact probability
that `o_a` exits in that round is `c w_a`; the restart probability is `1-c`.
Therefore eventual absorption is certain, the exit-owner distribution is
exactly `w`, and the prescribed payoff is exactly the same vector `v` for
every `c`.

**Proposition 13 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** The renewal
profile above is a

`(12 M c/kappa)`-terminal Nash profile

against all behavioral unilateral deviations. Consequently `v` is a uniform-
equilibrium payoff.

**Proof.** Pure-time extremality reduces the audit to one deterministic quit
time or `Never`.

First let `i` be unscheduled. Its deviation does not alter the opponent renewal
law. Every complete round which absorbs has exit-owner distribution `w` and
hence pays expected value `v_i`. In the last partial round before `i`'s chosen
quit time, at most `c` probability has been assigned to earlier exits or the
single simultaneous-exit atom. Replacing those bounded rewards by `v_i` costs
at most `2Mc`; on the remaining probability, `i` exits alone and receives
`s_i≤v_i`. Thus its gain is at most `2Mc`.

Now let `i=o_d` have lottery weight `w_i`. Replacing its behavior deletes its
own phase hazard. In one complete opponent round, exit masses before phase `d`
remain `c w_a`; those after phase `d` are multiplied by
`1/(1-alpha_d)`, because survival no longer loses the `i`-activation mass.
If `mu_i` is the expected payoff conditional on opponent absorption in such a
complete round, the support equality

`sum_(a≠d) w_a r({o_a})_i=(1-w_i)s_i`

gives exactly

`|mu_i-s_i|≤8 M c w_i/(1-w_i)≤8Mc/kappa`.

Indeed `alpha_d≤2cw_i`, `1-alpha_d≥1/2`, and the opponent absorption mass per
round is at least `c(1-w_i)`; subtracting `s_i` cancels the unperturbed weighted
sum and leaves only the post-`d` inflation factor
`alpha_d/(1-alpha_d)`.

Completed opponent rounds therefore contribute at most `8Mc/kappa` of gain.
The final partial opponent round has total exit-or-tie mass at most `2c`; all
remaining mass pays the solo value `s_i`, so it contributes at most another
`4Mc`. The sum is at most `12Mc/kappa`. Choosing `Never` omits the partial-
round term and obeys the same bound. This proves the pure-time estimate and
hence the behavioral claim. Sending `c` to zero preserves the exact target
`v`, so the fixed-target acceptance compiler finishes the proof. ∎

This construction uses only private Bernoulli choices and public survival. It
does not posit a public lottery: the exact owner distribution `w` is generated
by the sequential activation probabilities, and a failed round is publicly
visible as another all-Continue history.

#### Two-owner test

With two positive weights, support indifference forces

`r({o_2})_(o_1)=s_(o_1)` and
`r({o_1})_(o_2)=s_(o_2)`.

Thus the two singleton rows coincide in the two active coordinates. The cycle
reduces to the two-window refusal geometry of Proposition 11; only rare
two-player collision atoms can create error. So two owners do not yet produce
a genuinely new positive-preemption mechanism.

#### Exact three-owner test

Let the three singleton rows be

- `r({A})=(0,1,-1)`;
- `r({B})=(-1,0,1)`; and
- `r({C})=(1,-1,0)`;

and let every coalition of size at least two pay zero. No singleton row
dominates the solo vector `(0,0,0)`, but uniform weights give target zero and
make every owner indifferent.

Take phases `A,B,C` and the exact hazards

`alpha_A=c/3`,

`alpha_B=c/(3-c)`,

`alpha_C=c/(3-2c)`.

Each owner exits with unconditional mass `c/3` per round and the round restarts
with probability `1-c`. Direct pure-time calculation gives exact terminal
debts

`d_A=0`, `d_B=c/3`, and `d_C=0`.

For `A`, the negative `B` exit and positive `C` exit have equal mass after
deleting `A`'s hazard. For `C`, the negative `A` and positive `B` masses are
equal. Player `B` can gain exactly `c/3` by waiting through the `A` phase and,
conditional on survival, quitting before the unfavorable `C` phase; no later
time does better because complete opponent rounds have nonpositive mean.
Joint exits pay zero and add no collision gain. Thus the error calculation is
exact, not merely covered by Proposition 13's coarse constant.

This three-owner table is the minimal exact demonstration that rare renewal
can overcome positive preemption without one dominating anchor row. All
three-player games already have existence-level coverage through
`quittingGame_exists_uniformEquilibriumPayoff_of_card_eq_three`; the new point
is the explicit fixed-target clock mechanism and the actual-data boundary, not
three-player existence.

A narrow search found closely related checked infrastructure in
`AnchoredSoloPeriodic.lean`, `AnchoredCyclicRenewal.lean`, and
`SoloPeriodicBlockCompiler.lean`. In particular,
`isUniformEquilibriumPayoff_of_soloPeriodicBlock` consumes an **exact** finite
solo-periodic Nash--Bellman certificate with owner anchors and spectator join
caps. Proposition 13 instead starts from a limiting singleton-mixture
indifference condition and constructs approximate terminal profiles by taking
rare renewal. No claim of Lean formalization or of novelty over uninspected
consequences of that broader cyclic infrastructure is made here.

### 16. Proposition 10 export-gate and novelty audit

Proposition 10 is mathematically useful as an explicit clock, but its
uniform-existence conclusion does **not** define a new solved class relative
to the already checked instant-punishment producer.

Write `chi_o` for player `o`'s exact quitting punishment value. Simon's 2007
Lemma 3 states that if `o` is abnormal, meaning `s_o<chi_o`, then for every
`k≠o` one has

`r({k})_o≥chi_o`.

If instead `o` is normal, the definition gives `chi_o≤s_o`. Hence, for every
distinct `o,k`, the paper already implies the two-singleton bound

`chi_o≤max(s_o,r({k})_o)`.

Indeed the normal case uses its defining inequality, while in the abnormal
case Lemma 3 bounds `chi_o` by the second term. Proposition 10's hypothesis
`r({k})_o≤s_o` therefore forces `chi_o≤s_o`, exactly
`IsQuittingInstantPunishmentIR reward o`. Its outsider inequalities are
literally `IsQuittingInstantNoJoin reward o`. The checked theorem
`isUniformEquilibriumPayoff_soloReward_of_instantPunishment` consequently
already gives the same fixed target `r({o})` once the punishment-IR inequality
is supplied.

This last bridge has an important formal-status qualification. Simon's
`Literature.Simon2007.lemma3` uses the paper-facing `MinMaxQuit`, whereas the
production theorem uses `quittingPunishmentValue`; there is no named checked
adapter equating those objects, and production does not import `Literature`.
Thus the displayed raw-to-IR implication is a correct ordinary-mathematics
source comparison, not a theorem already proved in production Lean.

What Proposition 10 adds is a concrete nonstationary punishment witness and
the sharp transparent estimate `debt≤Delta/L` for a designated `k`, without
selecting a near-minimizing stationary punishment row. That is a quantitative
realization of the abstract instant branch, not a strict change to the
conjecture-facing existence boundary. The source comparison is:

- Simon (2007), Lemma 3 supplies the needed two-singleton min-max implication;
- `quittingInstantPunishmentWorks_iff` gives the exact abstract criterion
  against unrestricted behavioral deviations;
- `isUniformEquilibriumPayoff_soloReward_of_instantPunishment` already
  preserves the named singleton target; and
- the new calculation identifies one explicit finite-clock witness and its
  `Delta/L` rate.

Accordingly **no export packet is created**. Gate items 1--5, 7, and 8 can be
filled for the explicit clock: `CODEX_GAUSS` and `CODEX_CEDAR` supplied two
substantive reviews with falsification tests. But mandatory gate item 6 fails for a
special-case existence export: the claimed solved class is already covered by
the cited paper and abstract production instant-punishment result. The missing
Lean adapter is a formalization/interface observation, not new mathematical
existence scope. The explicit estimate remains here as a reusable internal
lemma and diagnostic screen.

### 17. Odd-cycle stress test for conditional stationary face gaps

The source audit of `CODEX_GAUSS`'s conditional face-gap theorem exposed two
different questions. Its displayed four-player table `r*` is already solved
by a sure-exit profile: every own singleton reward is zero, so even the empty
all-Continue set passes `isQuittingSureExitSet_empty_iff`; the alternating
pairs on its four-cycle are nonempty sure-exit sets as well. This overlap does
not imply that the general face-gap criterion is subsumed. The following exact
five-player completion separates the two mechanisms.

Let the players be `I={d,e,0,1,2}`. Define a zero-diagonal matrix `M` by the
cyclic core

```text
      owner 0  1  2
who 0       0 -1  2
    1       2  0 -1
    2      -1  2  0
```

and set every off-diagonal entry involving `d` or `e` equal to `1`. Thus `d`
and `e` have no nonpositive off-diagonal witness and leave the first normal
layer, while each core player keeps its `-1` successor witness forever. The
normal core is exactly `{0,1,2}`, and its principal matrix is the checked
cyclic block underlying `fourMatrix`. Consequently the same reindexing of
`fourMatrix_normal_standardQ` and `fourMatrix_normal_noHomogeneous` shows, in
ordinary mathematics, that `M` has nonempty nonhomogeneous standard-Q normal
core.

Let `pi` be the five-cycle

`d -> e -> 0 -> 1 -> 2 -> d`,

put `K=64`, and define the literal terminal reward table by

`r({owner})_i=1+M_(i,owner)`

on singletons, while for every `|S|>=2`,

```text
r(S)_i = 1       if i notin S,
         1-K     if i in S and pi(i) in S,
         1+K     if i in S and pi(i) notin S.
```

The nontermination payoff remains the project's zero. Every own singleton
reward is `1`, and

`r({owner})_i-r({i})_i=M_(i,owner)`,

so the normalized singleton matrix is exactly `M`.

Take the probability box `[alpha,beta]^I` with `alpha=1/8` and `beta=3/4`.
For player `i`, write `b=pi(i)` and let `z` be the probability that at least
one of the other three background opponents Quits. If `i` Quits, direct
conditioning gives

`R_i-1=K((1-p_b)z-p_b)`.

Throughout the box,

`169/512 = 1-(7/8)^3 <= z <= 1-(1/4)^3 = 63/64`.

If `i` Continues and opponent absorption occurs, a singleton opponent row
pays `1+M_(i,owner)` and a nonsingleton opponent row pays `1`. Hence its
normalized continuer value satisfies

`-1 <= V_i-1 <= 2`.

On the lower blocker face,

```text
R_i-1 >= 64 ((7/8)(169/512)-1/8) = 671/64,
G_i=R_i-V_i >= 671/64-2 = 543/64 > 0.
```

On the upper blocker face,

```text
R_i-1 <= 64 ((1/4)(63/64)-3/4) = -129/4,
G_i=R_i-V_i <= -129/4-(-1) = -125/4 < 0.
```

Thus this table satisfies Gauss Proposition 10's exact conditional face
signs. Its cruder Proposition 9 test fails: here

`H_i^-=1`, `L_i^-=-63`, and `C_i^+=3`,

so the proposed lower range bound is `-7>3`, which is false. Continuers are
not passive.

**Proposition 14 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** The displayed
five-player table has no pure sure-exit set and no owner satisfying the
instant no-join inequalities, while its normalized singleton matrix lies on
the nonhomogeneous standard-Q side and its conditional face gaps satisfy the
strict hypotheses above.

**Proof.** The matrix and face claims were just computed. For sure-exit sets,
the empty set fails because any player gains from zero by quitting alone for
`1`. A singleton `{o}` fails because one may choose an outsider `j` which is
not the unique predecessor of `o` in the five-cycle. Then `pi(j)` is absent
from `{o,j}`, so joining pays `65`, whereas continuing against `{o}` pays
`1+M_(j,o)<=3`.

Now let `|S|>=2`. If some `i in S` also has `pi(i) in S`, then its current
reward is `-63`; by leaving it obtains either `1` from a remaining
nonsingleton coalition or `1+M_(i,owner)>=0` from a remaining singleton. If
some `i notin S` also has `pi(i) notin S`, then it currently gets `1` and
gains by joining for `65`. Therefore stability would force

`i in S  iff  pi(i) notin S`

at every vertex. Such an alternating membership assignment is impossible on
an odd five-cycle. Hence no sure-exit set exists.

Finally, for any proposed instant owner `o`, choose the same kind of outsider
`j` with `pi(j) != o`. Joining `o` at date zero pays `65` to `j`, strictly
above its singleton target coordinate `1+M_(j,o)<=3`. Thus
`IsQuittingInstantNoJoin reward o` fails for every owner. ∎

This test shows that the conditional face-gap theorem is not merely a hidden
sure-exit or instant-punishment criterion, and that its strict extension over
the crude range test is nonempty. It does not claim exclusion from every
producer elsewhere in the repository. The exact named overlaps checked here
are the LCP producing side, pure sure-exit sets, instant punishment, passive
continuers, and the earlier range criterion.

### 18. The rare singleton cycle is exactly the homogeneous LCP branch

Proposition 13's apparent certificate has a pre-existing exact algebraic name.
Let

`s_i=r({i})_i`,

`M_(i,a)=r({a})_i-s_i`,

and extend the owner weights by zero to a simplex vector `w` on all players.
If `v=sum_a w_a r({a})`, then

`(M w)_i=v_i-s_i`.

**Proposition 15 (ordinary mathematics, source identification).** The floor
and pinning hypotheses of Proposition 13,

`v_i>=s_i` for every `i`, and `v_i=s_i` whenever `w_i>0`,

hold if and only if `w` is a homogeneous simplex-LCP solution for the
normalized singleton matrix `M`.

**Proof.** The displayed identity turns the floor condition into
`(Mw)_i>=0`. Since `w_i>=0`, pinning on positive coordinates is equivalent to

`w_i(Mw)_i=0`

for every `i`. Together with `sum_i w_i=1`, these are precisely the fields of
`SingletonLCPFeasible M`, abbreviated by
`HasHomogeneousSimplexSolution M`. ∎

This is also stated on the production side by
`fullHomogeneousWitness_singletonMixture`: a homogeneous witness gives exactly
the singleton-mixture floor and pinning facts. The checked homogeneous-matrix
producer already supplies stationary approximate equilibria and a fixed
uniform payoff. Proposition 13 therefore adds an explicit rare-renewal
terminal realization and its `12Mc/kappa` bound, but no new solved existence
class. In particular, its three-owner rock--paper test is the uniform
homogeneous solution of its normalized singleton matrix.

The exact route correction is now sharp. Any renewal argument which retains
one common singleton mixture and the same owner pinning conditions is confined
to the homogeneous branch. To cross into the nonhomogeneous standard-Q side,
one must keep positive-rate chronological asymmetry, nonnegligible collision
mass, or a genuinely nonlocal punishment state. Letting every activation rate
vanish erases precisely the information needed for that crossing, in line
with the checked collision-concentration bounds in
`UniformEquilibrium/Quitting/AbsorptionPath/CollisionConcentration.lean`.

### 19. Two owners collapse; three owners support an exact positive-rate cycle

The smallest positive-rate solo schedule behaves differently at two and three
owners. Consider first two phases, with owners `A,B`, positive hazards `p,q`,
periodic values `x^A,x^B`, and the exact owner anchors

`x^B_A=s_A`, `x^A_B=s_B`.

Bellman recursion at `A` first gives `x^A_A=s_A`. Recursion at `B` then gives

`s_A=x^B_A=q r({B})_A+(1-q)s_A`,

so positivity of `q` forces `r({B})_A=s_A`. Symmetrically
`r({A})_B=s_B`. Thus a two-owner exact solo cycle again has a homogeneous
singleton support; it cannot be the first escape.

At three owners, phase order makes the continuation mixtures genuinely
owner-dependent. The following four-player table is an exact test. Order the
players `(A,B,C,D)`. Its singleton rows are

```text
r({A}) = (1,3,0,2),
r({B}) = (0,1,3,2),
r({C}) = (3,0,1,2),
r({D}) = (0,0,0,1).
```

Among active pairs put

```text
r({A,B}) = (1/2,0,0,2),
r({B,C}) = (0,1/2,0,2),
r({C,A}) = (0,0,1/2,2),
```

and put `r({A,B,C})=(-1,-1,-1,2)`. For every coalition containing `D`, set
the `D` coordinate to `1`. Set active coordinates to zero when that active
player is outside the coalition. When an active player belongs to such a
coalition, give it `-10`, except that

`r({D,A})_A=1`.

This specifies all nonempty coalitions.

Schedule phases `A,B,C`, each with Quit probability `1/2`, with all other
players Continuing. Use the periodic value rows

```text
x^A = (1,2,1,2),
x^B = (1,1,2,2),
x^C = (2,1,1,2).
```

**Proposition 16 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** These data
form an exact `IsSoloPeriodicCertificate`. Consequently `x^A` is a
uniform-equilibrium payoff against unrestricted behavioral deviations. The
table has no sure-exit set and no instant no-join owner. Its normalized normal
core is the nonhomogeneous standard-Q cyclic matrix.

**Proof.** The Bellman rows are exact arithmetic:

```text
x^A=(r({A})+x^B)/2,
x^B=(r({B})+x^C)/2,
x^C=(r({C})+x^A)/2.
```

The owner anchors are respectively

`r({A})_A=x^B_A=1`,
`r({B})_B=x^C_B=1`, and
`r({C})_C=x^A_C=1`.

For the one strategically tight inactive player at each phase, the Quit and
Continue endpoints are

```text
A phase, C: (1/2)(1/2)+(1/2)(1)=3/4 <= x^A_C=1,
B phase, A: (1/2)(1/2)+(1/2)(1)=3/4 <= x^B_A=1,
C phase, B: (1/2)(1/2)+(1/2)(1)=3/4 <= x^C_B=1.
```

The other active spectator has Continue value `2` and Quit endpoint at most
`1/2`. Player `D` has Continue value `2` and Quit endpoint `1` at every phase.
Thus every join cap holds. Every player sees a positive opponent hazard during
the three-phase turn, so the admissibility condition uses the contracting
branch at every coordinate. The exact compiler
`isUniformEquilibriumPayoff_of_soloPeriodicBlock` now gives the all-behavior
conclusion.

For pure sure-exit sets, the active-only empty and singleton sets are broken
by solo value `1` and the designated `1/2` collision gains. Every active pair
has a member who gains by leaving for the preceding singleton payoff `3`, and
the active triple has a member gain from `-1` to a nonnegative pair payoff.
The singleton `{D}` is broken by `A`, which joins for `1` instead of zero;
`{D,A}` is broken by `D`, which leaves for `r({A})_D=2`; and every other set
containing `D` has an active member who leaves a `-10` payoff for zero. Hence
there is no sure-exit set.

The same designated collisions violate instant no-join for owners `A,B,C`,
while `r({D,A})_A=1>r({D})_A=0` violates it for `D`.

Finally the normalized singleton matrix is

```text
      A  B  C  D
A     0 -1  2 -1
B     2  0 -1 -1
C    -1  2  0 -1
D     1  1  1  0
```

Player `D` leaves the first normal layer because its off-diagonal row is
strictly positive. The active normal core is exactly the checked cyclic
`3 x 3` matrix, which is standard Q and has no homogeneous simplex solution.
∎

This is the first exact cycle in this notebook which crosses the homogeneous
singleton-mixture boundary. It remains a supplied certificate for a literal
table, not an arbitrary-game producer, and it may overlap broader cyclic
classification results not inspected here. Its durable role is to isolate the
minimal mechanism: three scheduled owners, fixed positive hazards, and
phase-dependent continuation values carry information that every rare common-
mixture limit loses.

### 20. Exact directed-triangle rates and the ambient outsider-floor cone

The source overlap can now be stated exactly. Let `A,B,C` be distinct scheduled
owners in that cyclic order, put `s_i=r({i})_i`, and assume that their
normalized singleton submatrix has the strict orientation

```text
        owner A   owner B   owner C
row A      0        -a_A       b_A
row B     b_B        0        -a_B
row C    -a_C       b_C         0
```

with all six magnitudes positive. Write

`Delta=b_A b_B b_C-a_A a_B a_C`.

If the hazards at the three phases are `p_A,p_B,p_C` and `q_o=1-p_o`, the
three exact owner anchors are neither an analogy nor an asymptotic condition:

```text
a_A p_B = b_A q_B p_C,
a_B p_C = b_B q_C p_A,
a_C p_A = b_C q_A p_B.                       (E)
```

**Proposition 17 (ordinary mathematics; algebra already represented in a
checked source).** Under the six strict positivity assumptions, `(E)` has a
nonzero solution in `[0,1]^3` if and only if `Delta>0`. In that case the
nonzero solution is interior and unique, and is

```text
p_A = Delta / [b_B(a_C a_A+a_C b_A+b_C b_A)],
p_B = Delta / [b_C(a_A a_B+a_A b_B+b_A b_B)],
p_C = Delta / [b_A(a_B a_C+a_B b_C+b_B b_C)].              (R)
```

Thus the closed cube has exactly the zero solution when `Delta<=0`, and has
the zero solution plus the unique solution `(R)` when `Delta>0`.

**Proof.** If one `p_o` vanishes, the three positive coefficients in `(E)`
force all three hazards to vanish. If a nonzero solution exists, every hazard
is therefore positive; no hazard can equal one, since the preceding equation
would have a positive left side and zero right side. Multiplying `(E)` and
cancelling `p_Ap_Bp_C` gives

`q_Aq_Bq_C=(a_Aa_Ba_C)/(b_Ab_Bb_C)`.

The left side is strictly below one, so `Delta>0` is necessary.

For uniqueness, rewrite the first two cyclic equations as

```text
p_B = b_A p_C/(a_A+b_A p_C),
p_C = b_B p_A/(a_B+b_B p_A).
```

Substitute them into
`p_A=b_Cp_B/(a_C+b_Cp_B)` and cancel the positive `p_A`. The remaining linear
equation in `p_A` is precisely the first formula in `(R)`. Cyclic rotation
gives the other two. Each denominator is positive, while denominator minus
`Delta` is respectively

```text
a_C(a_A b_B+b_A b_B+a_A a_B),
a_A(a_B b_C+b_B b_C+a_B a_C),
a_B(a_C b_A+b_C b_A+a_C a_A),
```

so `0<p_o<1`. Direct substitution proves `(E)`. This establishes existence
and uniqueness. When `Delta=0`, the product identity would force all `q_o=1`;
when `Delta<0`, it would force their product above one. Hence only the zero
solution remains in either case. ∎

The strict boundary assumptions are real. If exactly one magnitude in a row
vanishes while its opposite magnitude remains positive, its anchor equation
rules out an interior solution. If both opposite magnitudes vanish, that
anchor becomes vacuous and continua of degenerate solutions can appear. Thus
Proposition 17 is a sharp theorem for the strict sign chamber, not a statement
about its lower-dimensional faces.

For example, if `a_A=b_A=0` and the other four magnitudes equal one, the first
equation is vacuous and

`p_A=t`, `p_B=t/(1-t)`, `p_C=t/(1+t)`

is a continuum of cube solutions for `0<=t<=1/2`. This exact falsifier is why
the six strict inequalities cannot be weakened merely to nonnegativity.

At the determinant boundary itself, take the symmetric magnitudes
`a_A=a_B=a_C=b_A=b_B=b_C=1`. Then `Delta=0`, and multiplying `(E)` gives
`q_Aq_Bq_C=1`; hence the only cube solution is `p_A=p_B=p_C=0`. This is the
exact boundary where `directedCycleMatrix_hasHomogeneous_iff` supplies a
homogeneous singleton mixture instead. On the other side, for example
`a_A=a_B=a_C=2` and `b_A=b_B=b_C=1`, the product identity would require
`q_Aq_Bq_C=8`, so no nonzero cube solution exists.

The formulas are not new. In
`UniformEquilibrium/Quitting/Classification/ThreePlayer/CyclicCompiler.lean`,
the exact correspondence is

```text
(rightP,rightQ,rightR,rightS,rightT,rightU)
  = (a_A,b_A,a_B,b_B,a_C,b_C),
(rightAlpha,rightBeta,rightGamma)=(p_A,p_B,p_C).
```

The declarations `right_balance_one`, `right_balance_two`, and
`right_balance_three` are exactly `(E)`, and
`rightSingletonCycle_isUniformEquilibriumPayoff` is already a complete
three-player raw-table producer with arbitrary nonsingleton rewards. On the
LCP side, under the exact source-parameter identification
`(a,b,c,d,e,f)=(a_A,b_A,b_B,a_B,a_C,b_C)`, `cycleGap` is exactly `Delta`, and
`directedCycleMatrix_standardQ_and_noHomogeneous_iff` identifies `Delta>0`
with the strict standard-Q/nonhomogeneous chamber. The independent calculation
above adds the converse uniqueness statement for the hazard system, but no new
three-player existence class.

There is nevertheless a precise ambient-player extension. For any player `i`
define its margins against the selected owners by

`m_A(i)=r({A})_i-s_i`, `m_B(i)=r({B})_i-s_i`, and
`m_C(i)=r({C})_i-s_i`.

Let `Q=q_Aq_Bq_C` and define the three cyclic value rows by

```text
x^A = [p_A r({A})+q_A p_B r({B})+q_Aq_B p_C r({C})]/(1-Q),
x^B = [p_B r({B})+q_B p_C r({C})+q_Bq_C p_A r({A})]/(1-Q),
x^C = [p_C r({C})+q_C p_A r({A})+q_Cq_A p_B r({B})]/(1-Q).
```

For an outsider `i`, the three solo-floor inequalities are exactly

```text
p_A m_A(i)+q_A p_B m_B(i)+q_Aq_B p_C m_C(i) >= 0,
p_B m_B(i)+q_B p_C m_C(i)+q_Bq_C p_A m_A(i) >= 0,           (F)
p_C m_C(i)+q_C p_A m_A(i)+q_Cq_A p_B m_B(i) >= 0.
```

These are necessary and sufficient because their left sides are respectively
`(1-Q)(x^A_i-s_i)`, `(1-Q)(x^B_i-s_i)`, and
`(1-Q)(x^C_i-s_i)`. They define an explicit closed polyhedral cone in each
outsider's three singleton margins. Nonnegative margins are sufficient but not
necessary.

**Proposition 18 (ordinary mathematics, not checked in Lean).** Let `iota` be
any finite player set containing distinct `A,B,C`. If their singleton
submatrix satisfies the strict directed orientation and `Delta>0`, and every
outsider satisfies `(F)`, then `x^A` is a uniform-equilibrium payoff against
all behavioral deviations. No restriction is needed on any reward row of
cardinality at least two.

**Proof.** Equations `(R)` and `(E)` give hazards in `(0,1)`. The displayed
values close and satisfy

```text
x^A=p_A r({A})+q_Ax^B,
x^B=p_B r({B})+q_Bx^C,
x^C=p_C r({C})+q_Cx^A.
```

For the three owners, `(E)` gives the active anchors and floors explicitly:
the two values at and immediately before an owner's phase equal `s_o`, while
the remaining value exceeds `s_o` by `p_Cb_A`, `p_Ab_B`, or `p_Bb_C`, under
the corresponding cyclic relabeling. Outsider floors are exactly `(F)`. Every
player sees a positive hazard from a different owner during the turn. These
data therefore form a `BalancedSingletonCycleCertificate`. The checked
declaration `BalancedSingletonCycleCertificate.isUniformEquilibriumPayoff`
mesh-refines the coarse hazards, makes the collision error vanish, and covers
all unilateral behavioral deviations. Its finite reward-bound estimate is why
no pair or larger-coalition hypothesis is present. ∎

This must be kept separate from Proposition 16's stronger literal profile.
That table also satisfies the pairwise join caps, so its unrefined three-phase
profile feeds `isUniformEquilibriumPayoff_of_soloPeriodicBlock` exactly. For a
general table in Proposition 18 the exact solo-periodic conclusion would need,
at every phase `o` and spectator `i`, the additional raw inequality

`p_o r({o,i})_i+q_o s_i <= x^o_i`.

Those join caps are not consequences of singleton data. The balanced compiler
removes them by subdividing each coarse hazard and accepting an `O(1/m)`
collision error while keeping the target `x^A` fixed.

Finally, Proposition 18 is not merely a restatement of the literal example or
of the normal-core-cardinality-three theorem. Take four players and the
singleton rows

```text
r({A})=(1,3,0,-2),  r({B})=(0,1,3,5),
r({C})=(3,0,1,5),   r({D})=(0,0,0,1).
```

The triangle has `a_o=1`, `b_o=2`, hence `p_o=1/2`. For outsider `D` the
margin triple is `(-3,4,4)`. The three numerators in `(F)`, after multiplication
by the common factor eight, are `0,21,14`; its displayed values are therefore
`1,4,3`, all at least `s_D=1`. Thus `(F)` holds sharply despite one strictly
negative margin. The normalized singleton
matrix is

```text
      A  B  C  D
A     0 -1  2 -1
B     2  0 -1 -1
C    -1  2  0 -1
D    -3  4  4  0
```

Every row has a distinct nonpositive entry already in the full set, so every
normal layer is the full four-player set and the recursive normal core has
cardinality four. Arbitrary nonsingleton rows may now be supplied. The ambient
criterion therefore defines a reproducible full-core subclass. The narrow
search found no named declaration combining the strict triangle rate solver
with the generic outsider cone, although both component interfaces are already
checked. The finite Essential-APS cycle interface is stronger supplied data and
itself transports to the same balanced compiler; it does not supply this raw
rate/floor adapter. This is an adapter-level extension, not yet an independently
reviewed frontier theorem.

The equality face is individually necessary. At the same symmetric rates,
changing only the outsider margins from `(-3,4,4)` to `(-4,4,4)` changes the
three scaled numerators from `(0,21,14)` to `(-4,20,12)`. Thus the latter two
phase floors survive while the first fails; cyclic rotation isolates either
of the other two halfspaces.

### 21. The named full-core deadlock misses the cone in one exact halfspace

The natural conjecture-facing stress test is the checked matrix
`FullCoreDeadlock.deadlockMatrix` from
